| **Total** | **2,816** |

**Everything the X-24B demonstrated is now done by software, and the one thing that has not moved is the corridor, because the corridor is set by energy rather than by skill.**

There is a second answer as well, and it is the more radical one. **The X-24's entire difficulty was that no energy could be added after the approach began.** A booster that lands on its engine answers that by adding energy, which is not a better unpowered landing but a decision not to attempt one. **Both answers are in service and they are opposites.**

The field as it currently stands is gathered in the cluster subsections that follow these analytic ones.

### The Flare Is Now a Control Law

Unpowered precision landing is no longer a piloting achievement but a guidance problem with a published solution, for an unpowered reusable launch vehicle in [AL-Bakri and Kluever 2017][research_al_bakri_kluever_2017], for unpowered drones and gyroplanes in [Kim and Tahk 2019][research_kim_tahk_2019] and [Fang et al 2019][research_fang_2019], for fixed-wing aircraft in flight test in [Woodbury and Valasek 2016][research_woodbury_valasek_2016] and [Sedlmair et al 2022][research_sedlmair_2022], and for spacecraft in [Lunghi et al 2015][research_lunghi_2015] and [Robertson 2017][research_robertson_2017], with a review in [Pogosyan and Vereikin 2020][research_pogosyan_vereikin_2020].

**The milestone came thirteen years after the X-24B.** On 15 November 1988 the Soviet orbiter Buran flew one uncrewed orbital mission and landed itself on the runway at Baikonur, unpowered, in a crosswind, with no pilot aboard at any point. **That is the same demonstration Manke and Love flew, performed by a machine, at more than fifteen times the weight**, since Buran's dry mass alone was 62 tonnes [Buran][ref_buran] against the X-24B's 8,500 pounds.

**What the automation did not do is widen the corridor.** The bounds derived above are energy bounds and they do not care who is flying. Written out, the touchdown speed is a function

$$V_\text{td} = F\left(n,\ \frac{W}{S},\ C_{D_0},\ k_i,\ \rho\right)$$

in which **there is no term describing the pilot**. An autonomous vehicle arriving too fast still floats, and one arriving too slow still runs out of speed. What automation buys is consistency in hitting the middle of a corridor whose width is set by aerodynamics, in [Bottasso and Montinari 2015][research_bottasso_montinari_2015], [Ye et al 2015][research_ye_2015], [Zhou et al 2015][research_zhou_2015], [Ellerbroek et al 2016][research_ellerbroek_2016], [Ackerman et al 2017][research_ackerman_2017], [Lee et al 2017][research_lee_2017], [Lombaerts et al 2017][research_lombaerts_2017], [Schuet et al 2017][research_schuet_2017], [Tekles et al 2017][research_tekles_2017], [Fellah and Guiatni 2019][research_fellah_guiatni_2019], [Gabrys et al 2019][research_gabrys_2019], [Zaal et al 2019][research_zaal_2019], [Van Baelen et al 2020][research_van_baelen_2020], [Campbell et al 2022][research_campbell_2022], [Kluever 2022][research_kluever_2022], [Yu et al 2022][research_yu_2022], [Ma and Chen 2023][research_ma_chen_2023], [Altunkaya et al 2024][research_altunkaya_2024], [Catak et al 2024][research_catak_2024], [Hsu et al 2024][research_hsu_2024], [Schieni et al 2024][research_schieni_2024], [Autenrieb 2025][research_autenrieb_2025], [Sarah et al 2025][research_sarah_2025], [Yue et al 2025][research_yue_2025].

### The Other Answer Was to Refuse the Problem

**A propulsively landed booster does not solve the unpowered landing. It declines to have one**, as the powered descent guidance of [Sagliano 2018][research_sagliano_2018], [Wang et al 2019][research_wang_2019_2] and [Botelho et al 2022][research_botelho_2022] and the retropropulsion aerodynamics of [Marwege and Gülhan 2023][research_marwege_gulhan_2023] assume. The rest is in the cluster on propulsive and planetary landing below.

The comparison is worth making precisely, because it is a trade and not a victory. The X-24B carried no landing propellant and paid for that with a corridor four tenths of a g wide and no second attempt. A booster that lands on its engine carries propellant it could otherwise have spent on payload, and buys a hover, a divert and an abort.

**Which is better depends entirely on what is coming back.** For a stage that is mostly empty tank the propellant is affordable. For a vehicle returning from orbit with a heat shield already sized for the entry, carrying landing propellant through that entry is expensive, which is why the vehicles that come back from orbit still land the X-24B's way and the ones that come back from a few minutes of flight do not.

### The Configuration Survived, Uncrewed

The shape is still flying, in India's reusable launch vehicle technology demonstrator, in [Sivan and Pandian 2018][research_sivan_pandian_2018], and in the unpowered approach and landing guidance written for such vehicles, in [Kluever and Neal 2015][research_kluever_neal_2015], [S. Hameed and G. R 2021][research_s_hameed_g_r_2021] and [Yao and Xin 2023][research_yao_xin_2023], and it survived in the form the X-24B pointed at and not the X-24A's. **Vehicles of this class are now designed to land themselves on runways**, which is the capability the two 1975 landings were run to establish. The rest is in the clusters on the X-24 and lifting-body vehicles and on orbiters below.

**What did not survive is the pilot in the vehicle**, and with the pilot went most of the reason to care about handling qualities in the way the programme did.

### The Drag Is Computed Now, and the Base Is Still Hard

Base and afterbody flows are among the least forgiving things to compute, as large eddy simulation of bluff-body wakes in [Blanchard et al 2015][research_blanchard_2015] and [Tran et al 2017][research_tran_2017_2], hybrid methods in [Schmidt and Breuer 2015][research_schmidt_breuer_2015], nozzle separation in [Yaravintelimath et al 2016][research_yaravintelimath_2016] and turbulence-model work in [Wu et al 2017][research_wu_2017] and [Spalart and Garbaruk 2021][research_spalart_garbaruk_2021] show. **The separated region behind a blunt base is unsteady, three-dimensional and sensitive to the approaching boundary layer**, which is precisely the combination that steady turbulence models handle worst. The Hoerner correlation with a flight-corrected coefficient remains a serviceable engineering answer for the same reason it did in 1975.

### The Physics Migrated to Lorries

**The most active base-drag literature today is not aeronautical**, as the boat tails, deflectors and append devices for heavy vehicles in [khosravi et al 2015][research_khosravi_2015], [Her et al 2016][research_her_2016], [Lee and Lee 2017][research_lee_lee_2017], [Kim et al 2020][research_kim_2020], [Gao et al 2022][research_gao_2022] and [Xu et al 2022][research_xu_2022_2] and the platoon studies in [Tadakuma et al 2016][research_tadakuma_2016] illustrate, and as the road vehicle cluster and the bluff-body share of the base drag cluster below count.

A heavy goods vehicle is a blunt-based body whose drag is dominated by its wake, which is the X-24's problem in a different medium and at a lower speed. **One of the authors of the lifting-body base-drag analysis also reassessed the aerodynamic design of heavy trucks**, in [Saltzman and Meyer 1999][ref_saltzman_meyer_1999], drawing on coast-down tests of a box-shaped van, a two-axle truck and a tractor-semitrailer, which is as direct a statement of the connection as anyone could ask for. Projectile base bleed and boat-tailing belong to the same family.

**The reason the physics travelled is that the drag bucket does not care what the body is for.** Base drag falls as forebody drag rises whatever the body, so the factor of three at the optimum is as true of a lorry as of a lifting body.

### Lift Without a Wing, Computed

Aspect ratios near one remain awkward, in [Lian 2011][research_lian_2011], [Rockwell 2011][research_rockwell_2011], [D and Singh 2016][research_d_singh_2016], [Amin Boukenkoul et al 2017][research_amin_boukenkoul_2017], [Cao and Tafti 2018][research_cao_tafti_2018], [Obeid et al 2019][research_obeid_2019], [Okamoto et al 2019][research_okamoto_2019], [Qi and Wang 2019][research_qi_wang_2019], [Chandra 2020][research_chandra_2020], [Gildersleeve and Amitay 2020][research_gildersleeve_amitay_2020], [Kase et al 2020][research_kase_2020], [Barrett-Gonzalez and Wolf 2021][research_barrett_gonzalez_wolf_2021], [Chopra and Mittal 2021][research_chopra_mittal_2021], [Lin et al 2021][research_lin_2021], [Degani 2022][research_degani_2022], [Dutta et al 2022][research_dutta_2022], [Traub 2023][research_traub_2023], [Aerodynamic Performance Enhancement of Low Aspect Ratio Military Drone Aircraft Wing through Employing Leading Edge Tubercles 2024][research__2024], [Jiang et al 2024][research_jiang_2024], [Verma and Kulkarni 2024][research_verma_kulkarni_2024], [Berci 2025][research_berci_2025], [Eraslan 2025][research_eraslan_2025], [Lampropoulos and Sarris 2025][research_lampropoulos_sarris_2025], [Numerical Investigation of Impinging Planar Shock Wave Interaction with Axisymmetric Slender Body 2025][research_numerical_investigation_2025], [Degani 2026][research_degani_2026]. **The classical relations still do not describe these shapes**, because their lift comes substantially from crossflow and from vortices shed off sharp edges instead of from circulation in the ordinary sense, and that is the same reason the article had to take an efficiency factor from a source rather than compute one.

### Handling Qualities Became a Discipline With Numbers

The question of whether a pilot can work with a vehicle is now posed quantitatively, in [Hebbar and Pashilkar 2016][research_hebbar_pashilkar_2016], [Efremov et al 2020][research_efremov_2020], [Bidinotto et al 2021][research_bidinotto_2021], [Ivler et al 2022][research_ivler_2022] and [Lampton et al 2024][research_lampton_2024], with the rest in the handling qualities cluster below. **The lifting bodies' own handling qualities record belongs to that literature**, in [Manke et al 1970][research_john_a_manke_1970], [Kempel and Manke 1974][research_kempel_manke_1974] and [Kempel et al 1975][research_kempel_1975], not for their shapes but because they were difficult aircraft flown to a standard by people who wrote down what happened.

### What the Gear Sees, Measured Rather Than Assumed

The touchdown conditions this article computes at residual path angles are now extracted from the routine flight data of transport aircraft to predict hard landings, in [Ding et al 2020][research_ding_2020], [Xiangzhang et al 2024][research_xiangzhang_2024], [Dong et al 2025][research_dong_2025], [Bansal et al 2026][research_bansal_2026] and [Chu et al 2026][research_chu_2026]. **Hard-landing detection is a routine service, and the distribution of touchdown conditions across a fleet is a known quantity** where in 1975 it was an estimate.

### What the Survey Shows

**The aerodynamics have not moved.** A truncated base still costs a factor of the square root of three in lift-to-drag ratio, the flare still has a corridor bounded from both ends, and the energy is still spent exactly once.

**What moved is who flies it, and whether the problem is accepted at all.** The X-24B's pilots hit a fifteen-thousand-foot runway from a corridor a few tenths of a g wide, twice, and that was the demonstration. A modern vehicle of that class does the same thing on every flight and nobody writes it up. **And a modern booster refuses the problem entirely by carrying the propellant to add energy back**, which is the option the X-24B did not have and would not have wanted.

### Approach, flare and automatic landing

**The keystone's descendants.** Approach and landing guidance, the flare and its control laws, automatic landing of aircraft and drones, carrier landing, ground effect and the energy of the approach.

### Orbiters, spaceplanes and reusable launch vehicles

**The orbital vehicles that inherited the landing.** The Space Shuttle orbiter's approach and landing tests, aerodynamics, entry and handling, and the winged and reusable launch vehicles whose guidance now closes the same unpowered approach.

### Lift of low aspect ratio wings and bodies

**Lift at aspect ratio near one.** Low aspect ratio and slender wings, lifting-surface and slender-body theory, vortex lift and high lift, the aerodynamics behind a lifting body's polar.

**Of the 99 records in this cluster dating from 2015 or later, 20 name an aircraft, a missile, a projectile, a nozzle or another flight vehicle.** The rest study generic bluff bodies, and with the 64 road vehicle records of the same years they are the current base-drag literature.

### The X-24 and lifting-body vehicles

**The vehicle and its family.** The X-24A and X-24B, the PRIME entry vehicle that flew the shape first, the M2, HL-10 and HL-20 lifting bodies, and the later crew return and personnel launch designs that kept the shape.

### Entry and hypersonic flight of lifting vehicles

### Other aeronautical literature

**The residual, reported and not hidden.** On-subject aeronautical work that belongs to no cluster above, mostly flight loads and gusts, flutter, and rotorcraft and vertical takeoff research aircraft. **No further subject in it is large enough to name**, and dividing it further would describe the filing rather than the field.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating the X-24 through its landing gets three things wrong.**

**The vehicles were not landing demonstrators.** They were transonic and low-supersonic research aircraft that happened to land without engines because that is how they were flown. The X-24B reached 1,164 miles an hour, and a great deal of the flight record is about stability and control at speeds that have nothing to do with the approach.

**The base drag analysis is retrospective.** Saltzman, Wang and Iliff published in the 1990s and 2000s, drawing on flight data gathered decades earlier. **Nobody sized the X-24B using the factor of three derived above.** The relation explains the vehicle rather than having produced it, and this article should not be read as a reconstruction of the design process.

**The flare corridor is computed from a point-mass energy model**, with a constant load factor and a drag polar that is a parabola. A real flare has neither. The agreement to nine percent is encouraging and is not a validation.

There is a fourth, about attribution. **Two concrete landings do not by themselves settle a design decision** as large as deleting an orbiter's engines. They were one input among many, and the article's account of the causal chain leans on a programme retrospective rather than on the decision record.

## The Source Base

The research literature here is drawn from the NASA Technical Reports Server, from Defense Technical Information Center records reached through their registered identifiers, and from the Crossref index. **The research survey holds 2,816 research records**, counting the definitions in the Research list of the References, of which 930 are report primaries, being 33.0 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 1999.** Of the 2,775 dated records, 699 were published before 1976, the period in which the lifting bodies flew, being 25.2 percent, and 1,177 were published in 2015 or later, being 42.4 percent.

**The survey holds 9 records whose titles name the X-24 or its SV-5 designation**, 8 of them report primaries. Most are the programme's own record, from the full-scale tunnel tests of the X-24A to the X-24B's flight test reports.

**This article and the X-23 article share a vehicle family and only a minority of their literature**, because entry mechanics at Mach 25 and unpowered landing at Mach 0.5 are different subjects with different vocabularies.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The reports this article's argument rests on are report primaries under that rule**, among them Larson's landing statistics, the flight-determined lift and drag of Saltzman, Wang and Iliff, Dana and Gentry's account of the approach, and Manke and Love's report of the X-24B programme. **The rule does not reach the journal papers among the foundational sources**, such as Hoerner on base drag and thick trailing edges, so 33.0 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

### Thin Subjects

**Seven subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the landing flare | flare, flares, flare out, flared landing, flaring | 37 |
| the trade between forebody drag and base drag | forebody, fore body together with base, afterbody, boattail, boat tail | 2 |
| touchdown sink rate | sink rate, sink rates, sink speed, sinking speed, rate of descent, descent rate, vertical velocity | 2 |
| the energy management of an unpowered approach | energy management, energy height, energy state, terminal area energy | 35 |
| the float after the flare | float, floating, floats together with landing, flare, touchdown | 0 |
| the span efficiency of a low aspect ratio shape | oswald, span efficiency, efficiency factor, induced drag factor | 2 |
| the drag of an ablated or roughened heat shield | ablated, ablative, ablation, roughness, roughened together with drag, lift to drag | 3 |

**The flare is studied mostly as a control and piloting task.** Of the 37 titles that name it, 11 concern automatic flare, flare control laws or guidance, and most of the rest concern piloted technique, displays and visual cues. Two relate the flare to the lift-to-drag ratio, a 1949 analysis of lift-drag ratio and stalling speed and a 1968 study of flare requirements for glide vehicles of low lift-to-drag ratio, and they are the nearest the literature comes to the corridor derived here from energy.

**The forebody and base drag trade is the article's own analysis.** Two titles name the forebody together with the base or the afterbody, so the factor of three at the optimum rests on Hoerner's relation and on Saltzman, Wang and Iliff's flight data rather than on a literature of the trade.

**The sink rate at touchdown is almost never the subject of a title**, which agrees with Larson's statement that the lifting bodies' vertical velocity at touchdown was not instrumented. Two titles carry the terms.

**Energy management is a reentry and orbiter subject.** Of the 35 titles that name it, 18 concern the Shuttle, a reusable or entry vehicle, a boost-glide vehicle or an unpowered glide.

**The float, the span efficiency of a lifting body and the drag of a roughened heat shield have almost no titles of their own**, so the float after a 1.5 g flare, the Oswald factor of 0.6 and the roughness penalty at landing rest on the documents cited where they are derived.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** A record that shares a word with the subject and nothing else is excluded.

**The vocabulary of this subject belongs to other fields more than to aeronautics, and three cases are severe.** Flare is overwhelmingly the solar flare, and secondarily the gas flare and the flare stack of petrochemical engineering. Energy management belongs to power grids, buildings and batteries, and in the report archives it names, among much else, a manual on energy conservation in Navy family housing. Base is the air base, the database and the base station, and survives here only because base drag carries its own disambiguating word.

**Much of the Space Shuttle's literature is not about flying it.** Titles naming the Shuttle or its orbiter also cover communications, payload experiments, launch-site operations, waste handling and materials, and only those on its aerodynamics, entry, approach, landing and handling are admitted. Approach, landing and terminal area also name air traffic scheduling and airspace operations, lifting-surface theory also names turbomachinery cascades, and drag also names ships, hydrofoils, ordnance and spheres in creeping flow, and these are excluded.

**Other records that share a word with the subject and nothing else are excluded as well.** They include multi-criteria decision studies that match approach and criteria, combustion studies of flames held behind bluff bodies, road and ground vehicle dynamics, economics and management papers including three on an economic hard landing, particle and Stokes-flow mechanics, marine and ocean engineering, space plasma and planetary papers with no landing in them, energy and chemistry papers, studies of landing in sport and human biomechanics, medical simulators, pyrotechnic illumination flares, solar flares, and studies of pigeons, honeybees, a cormorant and insect flight. Correction, erratum, clarification, retraction and withdrawal notices, figure, table and supplementary-material records, peer-review reports and journal front matter are also excluded, because a survey counts research works and these are parts of works or editorial events. Book reviews, dictionary and other short reference entries, news items and meeting notices are excluded for the same reason, and so is a second deposit of a document already counted, such as an abstract-journal or reprint listing, a preprint or a set of presentation slides beside the paper itself.

**Three families that a shared exclusion list refuses elsewhere are admitted here because they are this subject**, namely hypersonic and reentry vehicles, because a lifting body is an entry shape, head-up displays for the approach, which belong to the handling qualities literature, and road vehicle drag, which is where the base-drag physics now lives.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept, 119 in all.** Boattails of artillery shells and bullets are kept because a projectile base is a blunt base. Cylinder and bluff-body wakes from wind engineering are kept for the wake physics, rotorcraft stability and the handling qualities of spacecraft docking for their methods, and a study of body and feather drag in a peregrine falcon for its drag measurement. A reader may weigh each accordingly.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, as reported by the accessible record.** The X-24A flew 28 times between 17 April 1969 and 4 June 1971, 18 of them under power, reaching Mach 1.60 and 71,400 feet. It was rebuilt as the X-24B, which flew 36 times between 1 August 1973 and 26 November 1975, 24 of them under power, reaching Mach 1.752, or 1,164 miles an hour, and 74,100 feet. These figures are from the flight log printed by Reed. On 5 and 20 August 1975 John Manke and Michael Love landed the X-24B on the main concrete runway at Edwards, Manke at a planned mark 5,000 feet along it. The approach technique, a flare of about 1.5 g from 1,000 feet to level flight at 100 feet followed by a float, is documented by Dana and Gentry for the lifting bodies of 1970, and the Flight Research Center's case for flying an orbiter unpowered was published in the same year. The X-24A is the vehicle for which X-23A was requested in November 1965 and refused in December, and which received X-24A in 1967.

**Flight-determined data taken from a published source.** The dimensions, wetted areas, base areas and weights in the vehicle table are from Saltzman, Wang and Iliff, as is the finding that full-scale base drag requires a Hoerner coefficient near 0.10 against the model-scale 0.029, the pair of optimum coefficients 0.1284 and 0.3852, and the measured tailcone-on lift-to-drag ratio of 7.5 for the Enterprise. The flight-measured maximum lift-to-drag ratios by trim are from the same authors' 2002 assessment. The X-24A's touchdown airspeeds, impact load factors and miss distances are from Larson's tables, and the means and spreads quoted for them are computed here from those tables. The X-24B's derivative comparison is Sim's.

**Engineering analysis, performed here and checkable.** The drag bucket and its differentiation, the result that total drag is three times forebody drag at the optimum, the cancellation of reference area from the lift-to-drag expression, the predicted lift-to-drag ratios for all seven vehicles, the square-root-of-three tailcone prediction, the approach angle and sink rate, the integrated flare, the load factor solved from the quoted touchdown speed, the corridor bounds, the energy accounting, the float after a 1.5 g flare, the matched comparison with the flight-measured lift-to-drag ratios, the thrust a shallower glide slope would need, the effect of ablated roughness, and the statistics computed from Larson's landing tables are all derived in this article from stated assumptions or cited tables.

**Inference, and flagged as such.** That the X-24B approached in a high-base-area trim is inferred from the fact that the resulting lift-to-drag ratio reproduces the documented flare height at the documented load factor, not from a stated flap setting, and the documented landing configuration of the X-24A had its upper flaps closed, with speed brakes available. The 1.19 g solved from the quoted touchdown speed describes a flare ended on the runway, which the documented technique contradicts, so it is retained as a property of the model and not as a statement of what was flown. That the X-24B's concrete landings followed the documented 1970 technique is inferred from its being the programme's pattern, not stated for those flights. That the two concrete landings caused the deletion of the orbiter's air-breathing engines follows a programme retrospective and is not established from the decision record.

**Assumptions that everything downstream inherits.** The Oswald efficiency factor of 0.6 is taken from the source's comparison curve. The flare load factor is not chosen but solved from the quoted touchdown speed, and the table shows what other choices give. The approach speed of 300 miles an hour is assumed and lies below the documented band of 270 to 300 knots indicated. The drag polar is parabolic, the flare is flown at constant load factor, and the vehicle is a point mass throughout.

**What the record does not settle.** The actual trim and base area on the X-24B's approaches, and its final-approach speed. The actual load factor flown in its flare. The touchdown speeds and miss distances of the two concrete landings, which are not in the documents read here, and touchdown sink rates for any of the lifting bodies, which Larson states were not instrumented. The base pressures below Mach 0.8, which Saltzman, Wang and Iliff estimated rather than measured. The growth in span from the X-24A to the X-24B, on which the histories and the flight-determined table disagree. The date of the decision to delete the orbiter's air-breathing engines, and so the weight of the X-24B's landings in it relative to the case the Flight Research Center had made in 1970. Several Air Force Flight Test Center reports, among them the X-24B flight planning and conduct report and the flight test and wind tunnel performance comparisons for both vehicles, were not retrievable from the Defense Technical Information Center for this article.

## Out of Scope

The detailed stability and control derivatives of both vehicles beyond the comparisons summarised here. The M2 and HL-10 programmes, which have their own histories and are covered in [Jenkins, Landis and Miller][book_jenkins_landis_miller_2003]. The XLR-11 engine, which the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1] article covers. The Space Shuttle Approach and Landing Tests as a programme. Lifting-body structural design and the structural detail of the rebuild that turned the A into the B. The full decision record on the orbiter's air-breathing engines.

## Conclusion

**A blunt base costs a factor of the square root of three in lift-to-drag ratio, and everything difficult about landing an X-24 follows from that one number.**

Three instead of five means an eighteen-degree glide instead of eleven. It means approaching sixty percent faster than best glide, on purpose, because the flare needs the energy. It means beginning that flare a thousand feet up rather than thirty, and it means a corridor a few tenths of a g wide with running out of speed on one side and floating off the far end of the runway on the other.

**The X-24B flew through the middle of that corridor onto fifteen thousand feet of concrete, twice, and an orbiter that had been designed with jet engines for its landing approach flew without them.**
